Learn more Publications ⚲ Back to news Statements Access planning for Ebola medicines moves ahead during clinical trials Gilead’s newly announced voluntary licenses could speed access to medicines currently under evaluation for treatment and prevention of Ebola BDV, as Unitaid and partners advance access roadmaps to help affected countries secure supplies if trials are successful. 17 November 2026 © WHO Unitaid welcomes Gilead’s announcement of royalty-free voluntary licensing agreements with Dr. Reddy’s Laboratories, Eva Pharma and Ferozsons Laboratories. The agreements lay the groundwork for wider access to obeldesivir, an investigational antiviral being studied to prevent Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD). If clinical evidence supports its use and regulators approve it, the three manufacturers across different regions will be able to produce and supply obeldesivir in the 127 countries covered by the licenses. Gilead is also expanding its existing licenses for remdesivir – an antiviral used to treat COVID-19 – to cover Ebola BVD, paving the way for wider access pending clinical trial results. While clinical trials are still underway, Unitaid, WHO, countries, regional and international partners in the Ebola response have developed access roadmaps for candidate Ebola BVD medicines, including obeldesivir and remdesivir. These roadmaps identify the steps stakeholders can take now to help countries secure supplies quickly when and if the medicines are proven to work. As part of this effort, Unitaid is leading the engagement with manufacturers of these two antivirals to secure access commitments to supply all countries in need. Through its funded programs, Unitaid is also supporting and anticipating interventions to prepare for supply and adoption of therapeutics and diagnostics so countries can introduce these key tools without delay if trials support their use. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

乌干达在 2026 年 暴发 之前的埃博拉病毒常备工作中超过刚果 - Bioengineer.org

Uganda Outpaced Congo in Standing Ebola Preparedness Before the 2026 Outbreak - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报《传染病与贫困》中发表的一篇范围界定审查比较了乌干达和刚果民主共和国在 2026 年本迪布焦病毒病 暴发 之前的防备政策。由 Augustus Osborne 领导的这项研究发现,乌干达拥有更健全的政策框架,解决了 52 个准备部分中的 47 个(覆盖率 90.4%),而刚果民主共和国则解决了 38 个(覆盖率 73.1%)。乌干达的政策包括具体措施,例如模块化培训计划、国家参考实验室和诊断标本运输网络。该研究强调了危机前卫生安全结构在制定流行病应对措施方面的重要性,并指出在行动特异性和后勤准备情况方面存在巨大差距,特别是在 监测 和隔离能力方面。研究结果基于对国家政策工具、暴发 记录和国际评估的彻底分析,没有对 暴发 本身进行直接测试或调查。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年本迪布焦病毒病 暴发 于 2026 年 5 月 15 日出现,该研究于 2026 年 11 月 17 日发表。该研究是使用 暴发 发病数据和先前存在的国家防备政策进行的。
地点与对象
该研究重点关注刚果民主共和国和乌干达,比较了两国的国家备灾政策和卫生系统。暴发 事件跨越国际边界,影响到两国。
事件
《传染病与贫困》中发表的一篇范围界定审查比较了乌干达和刚果民主共和国在 2026 年本迪布焦病毒病 暴发 之前的防备政策。该研究发现它们的卫生系统能力和政策框架存在显着差异。
规模、影响与风险
该研究评估了 15 个国家政策工具的 52 个详细的准备组成部分。乌干达处理了 47 个组成部分(覆盖率 90.4%),而刚果民主共和国处理了 38 个组成部分(覆盖率 73.1%)。该研究强调了作战特异性和后勤准备情况方面的差异,特别是在 监测 和隔离能力方面。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究基于对国家政策工具、暴发 操作记录和国际评估的全面审查。它不涉及对 暴发 的直接调查或测试,但提供了危机前卫生安全结构如何影响流行病应对的重要文件证据。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Uganda Outpaced Congo in Standing Ebola Preparedness Before the 2026 Outbreak - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A scoping review published in Infectious Diseases of Poverty compared the preparedness policies of Uganda and the Democratic Republic of the Congo before the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak. The study, led by Augustus Osborne, found that Uganda had a more robust policy framework, addressing 47 out of 52 preparedness components (90.4% coverage) compared to the Democratic Republic of the Congo, which addressed 38 (73.1% coverage). Uganda's policies included specific measures such as modular training schedules, national reference laboratories, and diagnostic specimen transport networks. The research emphasized the importance of pre-crisis health security structures in shaping epidemic response, noting significant gaps in operational specificity and logistical readiness, particularly in surveillance and isolation capacities. The findings were based on a thorough analysis of national policy instruments, outbreak records, and international assessments, without direct testing or investigation of the outbreak itself.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Wednesday, November 18, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Uganda Outpaced Congo in Standing Ebola Preparedness Before the 2026 Outbreak by November 17, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Uganda Outpaced Congo in Standing Ebola Preparedness Before the 2026 Outbreak Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram When the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak emerged simultaneously across international borders on May 15, 2026, the public health systems of the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda confronted an identical pathogen with no licensed vaccines or established therapeutic cures. A comprehensive scoping review published in Infectious Diseases of Poverty by an international research team led by Augustus Osborne indicates that pre-existing national preparedness policy architecture and standing health system capabilities differed substantially between the two sovereign nations long before the pathogen surfaced. By rigorously evaluating national policy instruments in force at the outbreak onset alongside external international assessments, the authors identified marked disparities in baseline surveillance, institutional accountability, displaced population inclusion, and standing isolation infrastructure, providing critical documentary evidence for how pre-crisis health security structures may condition operational epidemic response. The investigative team framed their scoping inquiry around the standardized methodologies of the Arksey and O’Malley framework, refined by Joanna Briggs Institute guidance and documented in adherence to the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews. Because the primary objective was to map a heterogeneous body of legal statutes, national health sector strategies, multi-hazard emergency guidelines, and independent technical evaluations rather than calculate a single pooled statistical effect size, the researchers retrieved fifty-seven verified sources following rigorous bibliographic checking. The evidentiary corpus encompassed fifteen national policy instruments in force on May 15, 2026—six originating from the Democratic Republic of the Congo and nine from Uganda—alongside seventeen operational outbreak records, International Health Regulations evaluation dossiers, and regional framework agreements. Each country’s standing instruments were searched, extracted, and charted against fifty-two detailed component requirements grouped into ten substantive governance and health system dimensions, with an eleventh dimension measuring operational specificity. A predetermined synthesis rule scored the coverage and modal specificity of each dimension into four categorical descriptors: adequate, partially adequate, inadequate, or absent. Across the fifty-two standardized preparedness components, Uganda’s legal and strategic policy instruments addressed forty-seven requirements, representing an overall national coverage rate of 90.4 percent, whereas the national instruments of the Democratic Republic of the Congo addressed thirty-eight components, yielding a coverage rate of 73.1 percent. When the research team evaluated the operational specificity of the individual policy provisions, the disparity became even more pronounced. Highly specific provisions—defined as concrete policy stipulations identifying responsible institutions, enforceable timelines, and quantified logistical resources—constituted 31.7 percent of Uganda’s 142 charted provisions, compared to just 15.2 percent of the 66 provisions charted from Congolese instruments. Conversely, provisions exhibiting low specificity, defined as aspirational intentions or generic assertions lacking procedural grounding, made up 15.5 percent of Uganda’s provisions but comprised 45.5 percent of the Congolese texts. Under the study’s standardized scoring metric, Uganda achieved higher policy ratings than the Democratic Republic of the Congo in six dimensions, including surveillance, isolation capacity, displaced populations, accountability, alignment with international standards, and operational specificity, while neither country was rated higher than the other in the remaining five dimensions. Discover more Study Biology Check Symptoms Track Outbreaks Buy Maps Read Travel Guides Surveillance architecture demonstrated the widest technical gap in standing operational specificity between the two sovereign neighbors. Uganda’s instruments addressed six of the seven surveillance components, supported by twenty-nine individual provisions of which twelve were ranked as highly specific. The country’s National Action Plan for Health Security outlined year-by-year modular training schedules in integrated disease surveillance and response, designated seven functional national reference laboratories, and mandated organized diagnostic specimen transport networks across at least 80 percent of administrative districts. Furthermore, Uganda’s strategic health framework established strict performance targets requiring detection of emergencies within 72 hours and laboratory confirmation within seven days, while operational guidelines for regional public health emergency operations centers instituted mandatory daily reporting aligned with the 7-1-7 timeliness standard. Although the Democratic Republic of the Congo also addressed six of the seven surveillance components in its multi-hazard emergency management plan, eight of its thirteen charted surveillance provisions exhibited low specificity, offering no operational case definitions and no enforceable timeliness metrics for electronic disease notification. Standing capacities for case isolation and clinical care revealed severe logistical deficits that materialized as acute operational challenges once community transmission accelerated. While neither nation had formally established minimum per-capita isolation bed allocations or standing surge deployment triggers in pre-existing statutes, their documented material commitments varied substantially. In the Democratic Republic of the Congo, the pre-existing national multi-hazard emergency plan quantified a theoretical national requirement of specialized case-management kits for 500 patients, but explicitly recorded that zero kits were physically available in national stockpiles at baseline. In contrast, Uganda’s policy provisions prioritized pre-positioning critical containment supplies across regional referral hospitals and detailed institutional mechanisms for specialized patient conveyance and dedicated ambulance routing. The integration of forcibly displaced communities into national health emergency architectures proved to be an equally decisive technical contrast between the two countries. Prior to the epidemic, June 2026 humanitarian assessments indicated that more than two million internally displaced people resided within Congolese administrative zones at direct risk from the pathogen, while Uganda hosted over two million refugees and asylum seekers, roughly one-third of whom were Congolese nationals. Uganda’s dedicated Health Sector Integrated Refugee Response Plan comprehensively addressed all five standardized evaluation components with high operational specificity, institutionalizing settlement health data into the centralized national health information system, funding community health workers within host districts, and formally embedding refugee emergency coordination under joint ministerial and United Nations governance. In stark contrast, Congolese emergency instruments addressed only two components, treating internal displacement as an isolated domestic hazard managed through the Ministry of the Interior rather than integrating displacement camp clinics into active epidemic surveillance networks. Despite widespread differences across domestic sectors, both nations exhibited critical, shared vulnerabilities in cross-border health governance and investigational medical countermeasure readiness. Neither country maintained standing bilateral protocols establishing binding cross-border case notification timelines, nor had either established standardized epidemiological thresholds to govern international movement restrictions with humanitarian protections. In the absence of bilateral legal treaties, Uganda enacted a unilateral suspension of international passenger transit across the border, an emergency measure contrary to temporary recommendations under the International Health Regulations and guidance issued by the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention. Regarding medical countermeasures, neither government possessed a standing regulatory instrument addressing Bundibugyo virus disease, as available vaccines and licensed monoclonal antibody therapies were specific solely to Zaire ebolavirus. However, Uganda possessed an established domestic approval framework linking institutional review boards, the Uganda National Council for Science and Technology, and the National Drug Authority under an emergency compassionate-use protocol, enabling rapid clinical deployment of investigational remdesivir, whereas the Democratic Republic of the Congo initiated its first experimental clinical trial on July 2. Independent multilateral health security evaluations conducted prior to the outbreak strongly mirrored the researchers’ documentary policy findings. The Joint External Evaluation missions executed under the auspices of the World Health Organization in October 2023 utilized an identical fifty-six indicator metric across both countries, awarding Uganda a mean overall technical capacity score of 2.75 points compared to 1.63 points for the Democratic Republic of the Congo. This represented a statistically robust mean paired difference of 1.13 points, with a 95 percent confidence interval spanning 0.83 to 1.42 points and a Wilcoxon signed-rank significance of P less than 0.001. Uganda scored higher across forty individual indicators, scored identically on thirteen, and scored lower on only three, with infection prevention and control representing the sole technical area where Congolese baseline scores exceeded Ugandan metrics. The largest institutional divergences emerged within national health emergency management, where Uganda averaged 3.50 against the Congo’s 1.17, and designated points of entry, where Uganda averaged 3.33 compared to 1.33. Similarly, self-reported capacity scores under the States Parties Self-Assessment Annual Reporting mechanism consistently favored Uganda by 16 to 31 percentage points between 2018 and 2025. A critical analytical contribution of the review lies in examining the reactive One Health response plan issued by the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s National Institute of Public Health on May 29, 2026, two weeks following the official outbreak declaration. Unlike the country’s ambiguous standing multi-hazard framework, this targeted provincial response plan covered thirty-seven of the fifty-two standardized components and achieved high operational specificity across 56.8 percent of its provisions. The reactive instrument clearly delineated four epidemiological caseload scenarios, established an aggressive 12-hour laboratory diagnostic standard, planned specialized ambulance allocations for each affected health zone, and guaranteed frontline staff financial allowances. The authors argue that this sharp contrast indicates the Democratic Republic of the Congo possessed sophisticated technical competencies in post-declaration emergency planning, but suffered from profound deficits in the enduring, permanent health system arrangements that must be financed, legally codified, and structurally maintained during non-epidemic periods, such as functional statutory emergency operations centers, quarantine laws, and standing material stockpiles. While the study demonstrates significant structural alignment between pre-existing policy completeness and subsequent containment outcomes, the authors emphasize essential methodological limitations inherent to documentary health systems research. A scoping review of policy documents and evaluation reports describes what institutional instruments mandate rather than verifying day-to-day implementation fidelity or frontline clinical efficacy across peripheral health centers. Furthermore, documented policy variations cannot be interpreted as the sole or definitive cause of the different outbreak trajectories, as significant unmeasured external confounders—including intense armed conflict, community distrust, remote geography, and prolonged undetected viral circulation prior to official notification—heavily shaped the operating environment in eastern Congo. The authors conclude that immediate health security reforms must prioritize establishing statutory emergency authorities, enforceable surveillance standards, and linked humanitarian health networks within the Democratic Republic of the Congo, while both nations must formalize standing cross-border governance protocols and pre-approved clinical pathways for investigational medical countermeasures. 吉利德签署免版税许可协议以扩大奥贝昔韦的使用范围 - 制药高管

Gilead Signs Royalty-Free Licensing Deals to Broaden Access to Obeldesivir - Pharmaceutical Executive
新闻简报吉利德科学公司已与三个仿制药制造商签署了免版税许可协议,以支持未来可能获得其研究中的 抗病毒药物 奥贝地西韦,用于埃博拉本迪布焦病毒病的暴露后预防。这些协议主要覆盖 127 个低收入和中低收入国家,包括当前 暴发 期间报告病例的所有非洲国家。由 ALIMA 和合作伙伴协调的 Ebo-pep III 期试验正在评估奥贝昔韦在活跃 暴发 期间高风险接触者中的疗效。吉利德还向肯尼亚、刚果民主共和国和乌干达捐赠了 38,000 多瓶瑞德西韦用于研究用途。虽然瑞德西韦已被捐赠用于同情用途,但其对埃博拉 BVD 的临床疗效仍未得到证实。该公司正在努力使制造和供应准备与正在进行的临床研究和监管流程保持一致,这标志着 暴发 准备采取积极主动的方法。这些努力是更广泛战略的一部分,旨在确保有效药物在显示出益处并获得监管部门批准后能够惠及有需要的人。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 11 月 17 日(发布日期),证据中未指定事件日期。
地点与对象
127 个主要是低收入和中低收入国家,包括在当前埃博拉 BVD 暴发 期间报告病例的所有非洲国家。
事件
吉利德科学公司与三个仿制药制造商签署了免版税许可协议,以支持未来可能使用其在研 抗病毒药物 奥贝昔韦作为埃博拉本迪布焦病毒病的暴露后预防药物。Ebo-pep III 期随机、安慰剂对照现场试验正在评估活跃 暴发 期间高风险接触者的暴露后预防效果。
规模、影响与风险
这些协议旨在扩大奥贝昔韦在 127 个国家的使用范围,目前该药物尚未在任何国家获批用于丝状病毒病暴露后预防或任何适应症。瑞德西韦已在多个国家捐赠用于研究/同情用途,但其对埃博拉 BVD 的临床疗效尚未确定。
应对、状态与不确定性
吉利德已启动与三个制造商的技术转让计划,并正在寻求更多合作伙伴以扩大产能。评估奥贝昔韦的现场试验仍在继续,该公司已向肯尼亚、刚果民主共和国、乌干达等国家捐赠瑞德西韦用于研究用途。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Gilead Signs Royalty-Free Licensing Deals to Broaden Access to Obeldesivir - Pharmaceutical Executive

News Brief
Gilead Sciences has signed royalty-free licensing agreements with three generic manufacturers to support potential future access to its investigational antiviral obeldesivir for post-exposure prophylaxis of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease. These agreements cover 127 primarily low- and lower-middle-income countries, including all African nations reporting cases during the current outbreak. The Ebo-pep Phase III trial, coordinated by ALIMA and partners, is assessing obeldesivir's efficacy in high-risk contacts during active outbreaks. Gilead has also donated over 38,000 vials of remdesivir to Kenya, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, and Uganda for investigational use. While remdesivir has been donated for compassionate use, its clinical efficacy for Ebola BVD remains unconfirmed. The company is working to align manufacturing and supply readiness with ongoing clinical research and regulatory processes, signaling a proactive approach to outbreak preparedness. These efforts are part of a broader strategy to ensure effective medicines can reach those in need if they demonstrate benefit and gain regulatory approval.

SHOW MORE Gilead signs royalty-free licensing agreements with three generic manufacturers to expand future access to its investigational antiviral obeldesivir. Advertisement The expanded access will also include the previously existing Remdesivir agreements. Stock.Adobe.com Gilead Sciences signed royalty-free voluntary licensing agreements with three generic manufacturers to support potential future access to its investigational antiviral obeldesivir as post-exposure prophylaxis for Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease. The agreements will cover 127 primarily low- and lower-middle-income countries, including all African countries that have reported cases during the current Ebola BVD outbreak.1 The move signals Gilead’s effort to get ahead of a currently ongoing outbreak by lining up manufacturing and supply capacity before clinical results are even in. The geographic scope of the new agreements mirrors Gilead’s existing voluntary licensing arrangements for remdesivir in Covid-19, with the agreements being designed to support manufacturing readiness, technology transfer, and supply planning, while a field trial evaluating obeldesivir continues.1 What do the agreements cover? The royalty-free licenses allow Dr. Reddy’s Laboratories, Eva Pharma, and Ferozsons Laboratories to manufacture and supply obeldesivir in the licensed territory, contingent on clinical data and applicable regulatory authorizations.1 Technology transfer discussions are already underway with these three manufacturers, with Gilead saying agreements with additional manufacturers are under discussion. Recommendations tailored to you Sign in or create an account to see articles, videos, and podcasts matched to your specialties. Sign in or sign up What trial is evaluating obeldesivir? Obeldesivir is currently being evaluated in the Ebo-pep trial, a partner-led Phase III randomized, placebo-controlled field trial coordinated by ALIMA and partners.2 The trial is evaluating the efficacy of obeldesivir as post-exposure prophylaxis for individuals at high risk of developing Ebola BVD.2 Obeldesivir has previously been evaluated in clinical trials for Covid-19 and respiratory syncytial virus, in addition to its preclinical filovirus activity. Related Articles M&A Roundup: Johnson & Johnson Acquires Ollin Biosciences, Eli Lilly Acquires Calytrix Bio, AmyriAD Therpaeutics Acquires AD04 Alzheimers Program, Sanofi Enters Collaboration with Seawolf Therapeutics Roundup: BioMarin Licenses Long-Acting Fabry Enzyme Therapy for $70 Million Upfront, Ferring to Acquire Fertility Biotech Gameto in Deal Worth Up to $1.38 Billion Roundup: Astellas Licenses IL-12 Fusion Protein From Audax in Deal Worth Up to $1.34 Billion, EVERSANA Acquires Health Economics Firm Estima Scientific The drug is currently not approved for post-exposure prophylaxis of filovirus disease, or for any indication, in any country.1 Jaime McCoy, vice president, Gilead Patient Solutions, Gilead Sciences, says responding to emerging infectious diseases, including Ebola virus disease, requires not only scientific innovation, but also early planning and collaboration to help ensure that effective medicines can reach the people who need them as quickly as possible. He continues to say, “These agreements reflect our commitment to preparing for future access while clinical research is ongoing, so that we are better positioned to respond if these investigational medicines demonstrate benefit and gain regulatory approval.” How has Gilead already responded to the outbreak? Beyond the new licensing agreements, Gilead has made direct product donations tied to the current outbreak. The company has donated more than 2,000 vials of remdesivir to Kenya for investigational use as treatment and post-exposure prophylaxis for people with confirmed Ebola BVD or individuals with a close contact exposure.1 More broadly, as part of its response to the current outbreak, Gilead has provided compassionate use donations of remdesivir to the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Uganda, and Kenya totaling more than 38,000 vials. These efforts sit within what Gilead describes as a broader approach to outbreak preparedness that includes clinical research, manufacturing and supply planning, technology transfer, and collaboration with governments, public health agencies and global health partners. The licensing announcement is one piece of a broader outbreak response. Gilead is also updating its existing voluntary licensing agreements for remdesivir to include filovirus disease, which would support potential future access should results from the ongoing World Health Organization-sponsored Partners trial support remdesivir’s use as a treatment for Ebola BVD, pending potential regulatory approvals.1 The clinical efficacy of remdesivir for the treatment of Ebola BVD has not been established, and remdesivir is not currently approved in any country for the treatment or prevention of the disease. Remdesivir has been donated for investigational/compassionate use in multiple countries, but its clinical efficacy for Ebola BVD has not been established.
Response, status and uncertainty
Gilead has initiated technology transfer planning with three manufacturers and is pursuing additional partners to expand capacity. The field trial evaluating obeldesivir continues, and the company has donated remdesivir to Kenya, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Uganda, and other countries for investigational use.

DRC Ebola outbreak still 'out of control' six months on, Africa CDC warns
新闻简报The Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) remains out of control six months after its initial emergence in Ituri, according to the Africa CDC. Over 11,200 infections and 5,500 deaths have been reported, with more than 80 percent of new cases detected outside contact-tracing lists and over 65 percent of deaths occurring in communities rather than healthcare facilities. The outbreak, driven by the Bundibugyo strain, has spread to Uganda, Kenya, and Nigeria, though these countries have managed to bring the epidemic under control. The Africa CDC chief, Jean Kaseya, warned that the situation could become the worst in history if not contained, citing risks of further cross-border transmission to the Central African Republic, South Sudan, Rwanda, and Burundi. Challenges include the lack of an effective vaccine for the Bundibugyo strain, limited treatments, and coordination issues between the DRC government and international donors. Despite international pledges of $2.9 billion, much-needed aid is still not reaching the worst-affected areas, and the Congolese government is working to digitize its payroll system to address staff payment issues. Research into vaccines and treatments, such as the Tumaini and Ebo-Pep trials, is ongoing.

The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), specifically the eastern region, including eight provinces, and neighboring countries such as Uganda, Kenya, Nigeria, the Central African Republic, South Sudan, Rwanda, and Burundi. The affected population includes communities and healthcare facilities, with over 65 percent of deaths occurring in communities.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

DRC Ebola outbreak still 'out of control' six months on, Africa CDC warns

News Brief
The Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) remains out of control six months after its initial emergence in Ituri, according to the Africa CDC. Over 11,200 infections and 5,500 deaths have been reported, with more than 80 percent of new cases detected outside contact-tracing lists and over 65 percent of deaths occurring in communities rather than healthcare facilities. The outbreak, driven by the Bundibugyo strain, has spread to Uganda, Kenya, and Nigeria, though these countries have managed to bring the epidemic under control. The Africa CDC chief, Jean Kaseya, warned that the situation could become the worst in history if not contained, citing risks of further cross-border transmission to the Central African Republic, South Sudan, Rwanda, and Burundi. Challenges include the lack of an effective vaccine for the Bundibugyo strain, limited treatments, and coordination issues between the DRC government and international donors. Despite international pledges of $2.9 billion, much-needed aid is still not reaching the worst-affected areas, and the Congolese government is working to digitize its payroll system to address staff payment issues. Research into vaccines and treatments, such as the Tumaini and Ebo-Pep trials, is ongoing.
Original source text (English)
Officially declared on 15 May, the Ebola epidemic affecting the eastern Democratic Republic of Congo ( DRC ) has continued to spread, reaching eight provinces across the country six months after it first emerged in Ituri. "We are all in agreement today to say that this outbreak is still serious, and is still not under control," Jean Kaseya, director-general of the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention ( Africa CDC ), said on Sunday. In an interview with RFI , he described the outbreak as "extremely serious". More than 11,200 infections and 5,500 deaths have been reported, according to government data. More than 80 percent of new cases are being detected among people who were not on contact-tracing lists, while over 65 percent of deaths occur in communities rather than healthcare facilities, Kaseya said. Ebola is most contagious during the late stages of the disease. "If we don't stop this outbreak now, it will be the worst Ebola outbreak that the world will have," he warned. A health worker wears full gear at a Ebola treatment camp in Butembo, Congo, Wednesday, on 30 September, 2026. While research into treatments and vaccines is progressing, efforts to contain the outbreak on the ground continue to face major obstacles. Driven by the rare Bundibugyo species of the virus, the epidemic is already the second-largest Ebola outbreak on record, after the 2014–2016 outbreak in West Africa, which infected more than 28,600 people and killed over 11,000. Inside Bunia's fight to protect families from DRC's deadliest Ebola outbreak The outbreak has also spread beyond the DRC over the past six months, reaching Uganda, Kenya and Nigeria. " Uganda has managed to bring the epidemic under control," Kaseya explained. "We had declared its end there – even though two cases, one from Kenya and one from Nigeria, had passed through Uganda." The situation in Kenya has also improved. Last week, the country announced that the final patient had been discharged from facilities used to monitor people who had come into contact with infected individuals. "We have also seen very good results in Nigeria ," Kaseya added. "Not all the identified cases tested positive; at present, the situation appears to be under control." However, the Africa CDC chief remains concerned about the continuing spread of the epidemic within the DRC and the risk of further cross-border transmission. "First, there is the Central African Republic , which is a major source of concern for me. Second, there is South Sudan , which also remains a major source of concern." He also highlighted the potential risk to Rwanda and Burundi if the outbreak spreads from the Beni-Butembo area towards Goma and Bukavu. "And, of course, there are all the other countries that could be affected indirectly," he added. Medical charity warns funding for DRC Ebola fight not reaching front lines Kaseya told RFI that the lack of an effective vaccine against the Bundibugyo strain remains one of the biggest challenges. A vaccine "should have been available for the past 19 years, given that the Bundibugyo strain has been known for that long," he said. He added that the absence of treatments and diagnostic tests specifically designed for the strain had contributed to the scale of the epidemic. Research into treatments and vaccines is progressing, however, with several clinical trials already underway, according to RFI's correspondent in Kinshasa, Paulina Zidi. The "Tumaini" clinical trial – named after the Swahili word for "hope" – is focusing on vaccine development. A group of experts from the World Health Organization ( WHO ) has recommended adding two new vaccine candidates specifically developed to target the Bundibugyo virus. Meanwhile, the Ebo-Pep trial is examining whether the oral antiviral drug obeldesivir can prevent the disease from developing in people exposed to the virus. The trial is being conducted at several locations, including treatment centres in Ituri managed by the medical NGO Alima . Africa CDC chief warns 'no country is safe' as Ebola reaches Nigeria Instability in eastern DRC, large-scale population movements and weaknesses in healthcare and disease surveillance have all contributed to the scale of the outbreak. Around $2.9 billion has been pledged internationally to help fight the epidemic. However, despite these commitments, much-needed assistance is still failing to reach some of the worst-affected areas, medical charity Médecins Sans Frontières warned last week. The organisation highlighted shortages of trained staff, ambulances without fuel and a lack of surveillance teams. Kaseya acknowledged that coordination between the Congolese government and international donors remains a problem. "We are not pointing fingers," he said. "We are saying it's a collective failure." He called on donors to release around $1.6 billion in pledged aid and work more closely with Kinshasa to ensure funds are used effectively and resources are not wasted through duplication. Kaseya also acknowledged that some healthcare workers had not been paid for months, adding that the Congolese government was working to digitise its payroll system to address the problem. For two weeks, the race remained too close to call but the win allows Democrats an even greater majority in the US Senate. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said there was "zero evidence" supporting theories online that recent outages affecting Microsoft, banks and flights are connected. The decision comes after California banned Waymo's driverless vehicles from operating on the state's highways following a series of traffic incidents. New Ebola Vaccine and Treatment Trials Bring Hope in Congo - News of Bahrain
新闻简报在刚果民主共和国 (DRC),针对 Bundibugyo 病毒的新 疫苗 和 治疗 试验正在进行中,这为对抗正在进行的 暴发 带来了希望。世界卫生组织 (WHO) 推荐了另外两种 疫苗 候选药物 ChAdOx1 和 mRNA-1469,用于 Tumaini 临床试验的评估,该试验的重点是 环形疫苗接种 以针对接触该病毒的人群。截至2026年11月11日,刚果民主共和国已报告确诊病例11,197例,确诊死亡5,471例,病死率约为49%。Bundibugyo 病毒是一个独特的物种,尚未获得批准的 疫苗 或 治疗,并且现有的 疫苗(如 Ervebo)尚未被证明对其有效。世界卫生组织还宣布计划将包括 maftivimab 在内的新 治疗 添加到 PARTNERS 试验中,该试验正在评估确诊病例的潜在疗法。虽然这些进展代表了进步,但 疫苗 和 治疗 的有效性必须通过临床研究得到证实,然后才能被视为成功。暴发 仍然不受控制,新的热点不断出现,不安全和对医护人员的攻击等挑战持续存在。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

New Ebola Vaccine and Treatment Trials Bring Hope in Congo - News of Bahrain

News Brief
In the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), new vaccine and treatment trials for the Bundibugyo virus are underway, offering hope for combating the ongoing outbreak. The World Health Organization (WHO) has recommended two additional vaccine candidates, ChAdOx1 and mRNA-1469, for evaluation in the Tumaini clinical trial, which focuses on ring vaccination to target people exposed to the virus. The DRC has reported 11,197 confirmed cases and 5,471 confirmed deaths as of 11 November 2026, with a case-fatality ratio of about 49%. The Bundibugyo virus, a distinct species, has no approved vaccine or treatment, and existing vaccines like Ervebo have not been proven effective against it. The WHO also announced plans to add new treatments, including maftivimab, to the PARTNERS trial, which is assessing potential therapies for confirmed cases. While these developments represent progress, the effectiveness of the vaccines and treatments must be confirmed through clinical research before they can be considered successful. The outbreak remains uncontrolled, with new hotspots emerging and challenges such as insecurity and attacks on healthcare workers persisting.
Original source text (English)
Breaking News Indian Envoy Visits Sneha Centre Stop Interfering, Stop Attacking Education Minister discusses cooperation with Arab Planning Institute Bahrain chairs 102nd GCC Olympic committees executive meeting Türkiye and ITU launch AI climate pledge at COP31 Indian Envoy Visits Sneha Centre Stop Interfering, Stop Attacking Education Minister discusses cooperation with Arab Planning Institute Bahrain chairs 102nd GCC Olympic committees executive meeting Türkiye and ITU launch AI climate pledge at COP31 Wednesday, 18 November 2026 عربيePaper News Views Videos ClassifiedsCampusCommunity More News Views Videos ClassifiedsCampusCommunity More SearchCtrl K Wednesday, 18 November 2026 Nov. 18 NewsViewsVideosClassifiedsCampusCommunity More NewsViewsVideosClassifiedsCampusCommunityMore Home News World 15 November 20265 min read New Ebola Vaccine and Treatment Trials Bring Hope in Congo Scientists are testing new vaccine candidates and experimental treatments against the deadly Bundibugyo strain of Ebola, as the Democratic Republic of the Congo continues to battle a major outbreak. Aalia Quadir Published 15 November 2026, 11:37 Link copiedShare Save The search for effective vaccines and treatments against Ebola has entered a significant new phase, as international researchers advance clinical trials targeting the Bundibugyo virus in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The latest developments offer renewed hope for affected communities, although scientists stress that further research is needed before any of the experimental options can be considered proven protection or treatment. On 4 November, the World Health Organization (WHO) announced that two additional vaccine candidates had been recommended for evaluation in the ongoing Tumaini clinical trial. The candidates, ChAdOx1, developed by the University of Oxford in partnership with the Serum Institute of India, and Moderna's mRNA-1469, are specifically designed to target the Bundibugyo virus. The recommendation represents an important development in the response, as these candidates are being considered for evaluation during an outbreak caused by the virus they were designed to target. Their entry into the trial is subject to national regulatory approvals. Two vaccine candidates enter the research pipeline The Tumaini trial is designed to evaluate multiple vaccine candidates through a single research platform. It uses a strategy known as ring vaccination, which focuses on identifying and enrolling people who have been in contact with confirmed Ebola patients. This approach aims to assess whether vaccination can help prevent illness and death among people at increased risk of exposure. The trial initially included Ervebo, an existing Ebola vaccine that has demonstrated effectiveness against the Zaire ebolavirus. However, its effectiveness against Bundibugyo virus remains unknown. The distinction is important because Ebola disease can be caused by different viruses, and protection demonstrated against one type cannot automatically be assumed to apply to another. According to WHO, four vaccination rings had already been established when it announced the latest recommendations, with additional rings being prepared. The inclusion of ChAdOx1 and mRNA-1469 is expected to expand the scientific investigation into potential protection against the Bundibugyo strain. Experimental treatments are also being investigated Alongside vaccine development, researchers are examining possible treatments for people already infected with the virus. The WHO-sponsored PARTNERS clinical trial, which began enrolling patients in July 2026, is assessing whether the monoclonal antibody MBP134, the antiviral medicine remdesivir, or a combination of the two can improve survival among patients with confirmed Bundibugyo virus disease. WHO has also announced plans to add another potential treatment, maftivimab, to the trial. Separately, a study examining obeldevisir as a possible post-exposure preventive treatment is underway. These medicines remain investigational for Bundibugyo virus disease. Their effectiveness must be established through properly conducted clinical research before conclusions can be drawn about their ability to prevent deaths or improve patient outcomes. A serious outbreak continues to challenge Congo The research developments come as the DRC continues to face a major public health emergency. According to WHO's epidemiological update published on 12 November, the DRC had recorded 11,197 confirmed cases and 5,471 confirmed deaths, based on data available as of 11 November. The reported case-fatality ratio was approximately 49 per cent, while 3,045 people had recovered. The outbreak has placed considerable pressure on healthcare services, particularly in affected communities where access to timely medical assistance can be difficult. In remarks delivered on 11 November, WHO Director-General Dr Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus highlighted both progress and continuing challenges. Although some areas were showing encouraging signs of slower transmission, new hotspots were still emerging and the outbreak remained uncontrolled overall. He also highlighted insecurity and attacks on healthcare facilities and response workers as major obstacles to containing the disease. Why the research matters A scientific illustration of an Ebola virus particle. The Bundibugyo virus is a distinct species of Ebola virus for which no vaccine or specific treatment has yet been approved. This makes the ongoing research particularly important. Although Ervebo has been used in previous Ebola responses, WHO has emphasised that its protection against Bundibugyo virus in humans has not been established. Its evaluation in research protocols is therefore essential to determine whether it can provide meaningful protection. The new candidates could help address this gap, but clinical trials must establish their safety and effectiveness before they can be considered successful. WHO expects initial findings from the treatment trials in the coming months, while results from the Tumaini vaccine trial are expected in early 2027. Hope, but no confirmed breakthrough yet Healthcare workers receive an Ebola vaccine in Beni, North Kivu, during the outbreak response in October 2026. The progress in vaccine and treatment research represents an important step in the international effort to respond to the outbreak. However, experts continue to emphasise that research alone will not bring the epidemic under control. Early diagnosis, appropriate supportive care, contact tracing, infection prevention, safe and dignified burials, and cooperation with affected communities remain essential parts of the response. For communities affected by the outbreak, the trials offer the possibility of better protection and treatment in the future. Their ultimate significance, however, will depend on the scientific evidence they produce. For now, the developments represent cautious optimism rather than a confirmed medical breakthrough, as researchers continue working towards effective tools against one of the most challenging Ebola virus diseases. 专为 Bundibugyo 病毒开发的两种 疫苗、ChAdOx1 和 mRNA-1469 将加入刚果民主共和国的 Tumaini 临床试验。肯尼亚也计划参加试验。
规模、影响与风险
刚果民主共和国 暴发 已记录超过 10,500 例确诊病例和 5,126 例死亡。该试验旨在评估 疫苗 针对本迪布焦病毒的安全性、免疫原性和有效性。肯尼亚确诊病例康复率为78%。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗 仍在调查中,将其纳入试验并不能证明其功效或批准公众使用。该试验使用 环形疫苗接种 来针对高风险接触者。试验正在进行中,供应是下一个障碍。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola: 2 vaccines to join DRC’s Tumaini trial as Kenya eyes Ksh4B study - K24 Digital

News Brief
In November 2026, two vaccines, ChAdOx1 and mRNA-1469, developed specifically for the Bundibugyo species of Ebola virus, are set to join the Tumaini clinical trial in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). The DRC has been battling an Ebola outbreak since May 15, 2026, which has recorded over 10,500 confirmed cases and 5,126 deaths, making it the largest in the country’s history. The trial, which began in Bunia, Eastern DRC, on October 26, 2026, uses ring vaccination to target high-risk contacts. Kenya, which had an imported outbreak of Bundibugyo Virus Disease (BVD), plans to participate in the trial. The vaccines are still under investigation, and their inclusion does not guarantee approval for public use. The trial aims to assess their safety, immunogenicity, and efficacy. In Kenya, a recovery rate of 78% was reported for nine confirmed cases linked to an imported outbreak. The World Health Organization (WHO) and other partners are supporting the trial, and supply is the next major challenge.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content ePaper Football Leagues 26/27 Search K24 Digital News Entertainment Lifestyle Sports Health & Fitness Ebola: 2 vaccines to join DRC’s Tumaini trial as Kenya eyes Ksh4B study Willow Health 15th November, 2026 04:51 PM Updated 15th November, 2026 08:33 PM A medical officer at the lab. PHOTO/WHO For almost six months, the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) has fought an Ebola outbreak with no approved vaccine for the virus driving it. Since the outbreak was declared on May 15, 2026, it has recorded more than 10,500 confirmed cases and 5,126 deaths, making it the largest in the country’s history. That gap may now narrow as two vaccine candidates developed specifically to protect against the Bundibugyo species of Ebola are set to join the ongoing Tumaini clinical trial in the DRC. It will be the first time vaccines designed for Bundibugyo virus are tested during an outbreak of the disease. The World Health Organization (WHO) announced the decision on November 4, 2026. Its independent Technical Advisory Group on Candidate Vaccine Prioritization recommended the pair after reviewing available preclinical and early clinical evidence. The two are ChAdOx1, developed by the University of Oxford in partnership with the Serum Institute of India, and Moderna’s mRNA-1469. Both build on technology proven during Covid-19. ChAdOx1 uses a modified chimpanzee adenovirus, the platform behind Oxford’s Covid-19 vaccine, to deliver genetic instructions for a protein on the surface of Bundibugyo virus. mRNA-1469 carries similar instructions in messenger RNA packaged in tiny lipid particles. According to Gavi, the Vaccine Alliance, both produced encouraging results in animal studies, and early trials in the United Kingdom and Canada suggest they can safely stimulate immune responses in humans. Tumaini, which means hope in Kiswahili, began in Bunia, where the first participant received a vaccine dose on October 26, 2026 WHO informed the developers on November 2, 2026, and the candidates will enter the trial only after receiving the necessary national approvals. “The fact that these vaccines are ready to enter clinical trials is a testament to the power of science and partnership,” said WHO Director-General Dr Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus. He thanked the scientists, health workers and partners involved, and “above all the trial participants, whose involvement would generate evidence that could help save lives.” Tumaini, which means hope in Kiswahili, began in Bunia, Eastern DRC, where the first participant received a vaccine dose on October 26, 2026. The Phase 2/3 trial tests several vaccine candidates against Bundibugyo virus within a single research framework, measuring their safety, immunogenicity and efficacy. Ervebo was the first vaccine to enter the trial. It is already licensed to prevent Ebola virus disease caused by Zaire ebolavirus, but its effectiveness against Bundibugyo virus has yet to be established. ChAdOx1 and mRNA-1469, already identified in efforts to widen Tumaini’s range of candidates, will give researchers options built specifically against the Bundibugyo species. The trial relies on ring vaccination where instead of targeting a whole population, teams identify people who have been in contact with someone confirmed to have Bundibugyo Virus Disease (BVD), and who are therefore at high risk of infection, and enrol them in vaccination rings. WHO says four rings have been established, with several more being prepared. They include contacts of people who fell ill or died. The approach draws on the West African outbreak about a decade ago, when a landmark trial in Guinea established Ervebo’s efficacy against Zaire ebolavirus. That experience lets Tumaini to assess vaccines while the response is still underway, rather than waiting for the outbreak to end. The burial of an Ebola victim. PHOTO/https://www.facebook.com/IFRC Gavi has separately pledged up to US$40 million (Ksh5.2 billion) for manufacturing readiness For affected communities, the research could eventually offer a new tool against a species that has challenged existing Ebola vaccine efforts. The candidates, however, remain under investigation. Their place in the trial is not proof that they work, nor that they are approved for public use. Even a successful vaccine will not help people already infected. The PARTNERS trial, launched in July 2026, is evaluating whether the monoclonal antibody MBP134, the antiviral drug remdesivir or a combination of both can improve survival among people with confirmed BVD. It is expected to expand to include a further candidate, maftivimab. A complementary post-exposure prophylaxis study involving obeldevisir is also underway. Through its Research and Development Blueprint and Health Emergencies Programme, WHO helps develop protocols, gives strategic and technical advice, coordinates partners and supports the studies within the outbreak response. The Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale (INRB), the DRC’s national public health and biomedical research institute, leads Tumaini. It is supported by the University of Antwerp in Belgium, WHO, Epicentre, Médecins Sans Frontières, the Institute of Tropical Medicine of Antwerp and the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC). Investment from the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI), which Gavi says exceeds US$75 million (Ksh9.8 billion), enabled the candidates’ rapid development. Gavi has separately pledged up to US$40 million (Ksh5.2 billion) for manufacturing readiness, so successful vaccines can be produced at scale. IAVI has received US$36 million (Ksh4.7 billion) to begin a trial in Kenya in January 2027 Kenya, which contained an imported outbreak of BVD, plans to join the search. Speaking in Nairobi on Wednesday November 11, as the country announced its official countdown to Ebola-free status, Director General for Health Dr Patrick Amoth said “Kenya will be participating in the Tumaini trial”. He added that IAVI had received US$36 million (Ksh4.7 billion) to begin a trial in Kenya in January 2027. IAVI announced on October 28 that the US Biomedical Advanced Research and Development Authority (BARDA) had awarded it the sum, part of a contract worth up to US$99 million (Ksh12.9 billion), to take a single-dose vaccine built on Ervebo’s rVSV platform through a Phase 1 trial. Kenya’s own patients relied on the experimental drugs now under study in PARTNERS. “We don’t have any specific vaccine or therapeutic for this strain of Ebola virus, the Bundibugyo virus,” Dr Amoth said. Each person with BVD received a single infusion of MBP134, followed by a ten-day course of remdesivir, an antiviral widely used against Covid-19. Supportive care included nutrition, blood transfusions and ventilatory support where needed. Dr Loice Ombajo, an infectious disease specialist at the University of Nairobi, said in an earlier interview with Willow Health Media that the drugs remain experimental because “we need to collect sufficient data on the effectiveness and safety on a larger scale to inform future outbreaks.” She stressed treating early, before virus levels rise too high for the drugs to be effective. On November 11, Health Cabinet Secretary Aden Duale announced the discharge of the last person with BVD under treatment. Of nine confirmed cases, all linked to one imported case, seven recovered and two died, a recovery rate Dr Amoth put at 78%. All 135 contacts completed monitoring, and there has been no community transmission. The response has entered a recovery and monitoring phase. If no new case emerges during the 42-day countdown that began on November 12, Kenya will be declared Ebola-free on December 23, 2026. Two health workers in full PPE disinfect one another Gavi is funding production before results are known and hopes to build a global Bundibugyo stockpile Treatment findings are expected in the coming months and vaccine results in early 2027, showing whether the candidates can safely protect people against Bundibugyo virus. Turning a candidate into real protection needs deliberate choices now. In a joint position paper published on October 23, the European Medicines Agency’s Emergency Task Force and the African Medicines Agency said Bundibugyo candidates “should move through the different clinical trial phases as quickly as possible” without compromising participant safety. They favour a single-dose vaccine because it “would be easier to deploy rapidly during an outbreak”. They also back early trials in Africa, platform trials testing several candidates through ring vaccination, and early talks with African regulators. Supply is the next hurdle. Because manufacturers have little incentive to make vaccines for rare outbreaks, Gavi is funding production before results are known and hopes to build a global Bundibugyo stockpile. WHO is expected to grant Emergency Use Listing or prequalification once evidence allows. Dr Amoth sees Kenya’s role in the same light. “We are part of the international community working together to be able to ensure that we have therapeutics or vaccines against this disease and other emerging pathogens of international concern,” he said. Dr Tedros was clear that vaccines alone will not end the outbreak, though they “could become a powerful new tool for bringing it under control.” Their impact will rest on the wider response and the health workers, researchers and communities who carry it. Author Willow Health W.H.M. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

刚果民主共和国埃博拉病毒暴发:武装团体引发的骚乱阻碍了应对 - 联合国新闻

DR Congo Ebola outbreak: Unrest fuelled by armed groups holds back response - UN News
新闻简报The Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) continues to spread, particularly in rural and hard-to-reach areas, as armed groups like the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF) obstruct response efforts. The Bundibugyo virus is the specific strain involved, with the World Health Organization (WHO) reporting 400 to 500 new cases per week. Despite progress in containing transmission in previous hotspots, the outbreak remains a significant public health challenge. The situation is further complicated by violent unrest, attacks on healthcare workers and facilities, and a concurrent humanitarian crisis marked by acute food insecurity affecting nearly 29 million people. The WHO and UN responders are working to strengthen prevention efforts and build trust with communities, but the ongoing conflict and insecurity hinder these initiatives. The outbreak, declared in May 2026, has resulted in over 11,000 confirmed cases and more than 5,000 deaths, making it the largest yet in the DRC. The humanitarian emergency is exacerbated by persistent conflict and large-scale displacement, with food prices rising and families being cut off from their livelihoods. The WHO has condemned attacks on frontline workers and emphasized the need for continued access and resources to address the crisis.
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发于2026年5月宣布并继续传播。最新更新日期为 2026 年 11 月 13 日。每周 400 至 500 例病例的趋势仍在持续。
地点与对象
暴发正在刚果民主共和国(DRC)的东部和东北部省份发生,具体包括伊图里、北基伍和南基伍省。刚果民主共和国超过29万人面临严重的粮食不安全问题,其中大多数位于冲突和埃博拉病毒影响地区。
事件
刚果民主共和国的埃博拉病毒 暴发 正在农村和难以到达的地区蔓延,民主同盟军 (ADF) 等武装团体对平民、卫生工作者和设施的袭击阻碍了应对工作。本迪布焦病毒是所涉及的具体毒株,截至 2026 年 11 月,世界卫生组织报告每周有 400 至 500 例病例。
规模、影响与风险
已确诊病例超过11,000例,死亡人数超过5,000人。暴发 的传播速度比以往任何一次都快,持续的暴力、流离失所和粮食不安全加剧了这一风险。世界卫生组织指出,预防工作已导致先前热点地区的 传播 大幅减少。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织和联合国应对人员正在努力遏制 暴发,但武装团体的暴力骚乱和袭击带来了重大挑战。接触者追踪和社区参与正在进行中,但这些努力受到安全局势的阻碍。局势仍未解决,需要持续的准入和资源。

DR Congo Ebola outbreak: Unrest fuelled by armed groups holds back response - UN News

News Brief
The Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) continues to spread, particularly in rural and hard-to-reach areas, as armed groups like the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF) obstruct response efforts. Facebook Twitter Print Email DR Congo Ebola outbreak: Unrest fuelled by armed groups holds back response By Daniel Johnson, Geneva 13 November 2026 Health The Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) continues to spread amid repeated and ongoing deadly attacks on civilians and frontline health workers by armed groups that are obstructing efforts to stop transmission, UN responders said on Friday. In an update, the UN Senior Ebola Response Coordinator, Julien Harneis, stressed that more people are being killed by Allied Democratic Forces (ADF) fighters than by Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus in recent days. And yet attacks on “tens of villages" across the south of Ituri and North Kivu provinces go "unnoticed", Mr. Harneis maintained. “People are scared, people are furious at the lack of action on this...following up contact tracing in areas where ADF operating is a very hazardous idea.” Up to 500 infections per week Echoing that message, the UN World Health Organization (WHO)’s Dr Chikwe Ihekweazu, Executive Director of WHO’s Health Emergencies Programme, said that despite much progress in containing transmission within eastern DRC, about 400 to 500 cases of infection are still being identified every week. Tweet URL This trend is linked to the emergence of cases in rural and hard-to-reach areas. In more positive developments, the WHO official noted that prevention efforts have led to “a significant reduction” in transmission in previous hotspots, in particular Bunia, Mongbwalu, Rampara and Nizi, all in Ituri province in eastern DRC. Nonetheless, the violent unrest across the vast area impacted by Ebola creates huge challenges for responders. “We’ve seen so many attacks on healthcare, attacks on communities as well, attacks on people providing care, and people losing their lives, nurses in burials,” Dr Ihekweazu explained. “So, we need to keep working with the community, and this is very important. And working with the specific groups instigating these attacks.” Disease threats in addition to attacks Communities are already facing outbreaks of diseases, malnutrition and displacement driven by decades of violent unrest fuelled by dozens of armed groups that remain active in the resource-rich region. Earlier this week, WHO condemned attacks against frontline healthcare workers and community awareness officers, including in Cantine-Aloya in North Kivu that killed 66 civilians including three health workers. Three health facilities were also looted. Two weeks ago, a nurse was also killed and another health worker badly injured when they were attacked while responding to an Ebola alert in Tshopo province. Tedros appeal Other attacks have included an arson attempt on a treatment centre and stones thrown at a vehicle used for conducting safe and dignified burials, an indication of the need to continue to build trust with extremely vulnerable communities. “We cannot focus only on Ebola. Some people in the affected communities have said told me personally that Ebola is a lesser evil compared to some of the other threats they face,” WHO Director-General Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus stressed earlier this week. A massive humanitarian emergency The DRC Ebola outbreak was declared in May and has since become the country's largest yet as it continues to spread faster than any previous outbreak across the vast east and northeast provinces. To date, more than 11,000 cases have been confirmed and more than 5,000 people have died. In a related development, the UN World Food Programme (WFP) warned that nearly 29 million people in the DRC face acute food insecurity, with most in the conflict and Ebola-affected eastern and northeastern provinces. That number is likely to continue to increase, said Jean-Martin Bauer, WFP Director of Food Security and Nutrition Analysis. Briefing journalists via video in Geneva, Mr. Bauer stressed that conflict is driving the hunger crisis, in addition to the Ebola outbreak. “What we need right now is access, and we need resources to avoid the situation becoming more difficult in the DRC,” he said. Hunger emergency Latest UN-backed food insecurity expert assessments indicate that between January and June 2027, 30.3 million people will be in crisis levels of acute food insecurity or worse, with 4.2 million of this number experiencing “emergency levels” of hunger and the most acute needs in North Kivu, South Kivu and Ituri provinces “driven by persistent conflict and large-scale displacement”. Mr. Bauer noted that Ebola is just one of many disease outbreaks in DRC, but that it is compounding the hunger crisis for communities already struggling to eat. “Families are being displaced, they’re being cut off from their farms, from their livelihoods,” he explained. “Food prices are rising, making affording a meal quite complicated for too many households.” Ebola Facebook Twitter Print Email Back to top

The Ebola outbreak in the DRC is spreading in rural and hard-to-reach areas, with attacks on civilians, health workers, and facilities by armed groups like the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF) hindering response efforts. 在埃博拉病毒中幸存后,刚果民主共和国医生重申抗击这种疾病的誓言

After surviving Ebola, DR Congo doctors renew vow to fight the disease
新闻简报在刚果民主共和国 (DRC),自 2026 年 5 月以来,正在流行的埃博拉病毒 暴发 已夺走 5,500 多人的生命,北基伍省是受灾最严重的地区之一。隔离 24 天后从埃博拉病毒中幸存的医生 Kinzi Divin 和通过直接接触患者感染病毒的护士 Muhindo Mbafumoja Shukuru 强调了一线工作人员面临的风险。截至2026年11月14日,由本迪布焦埃博拉病毒引起的暴发已导致北基伍省确诊病例11,376例,死亡5,568例,病死率为55.6%。尽管造成了人员伤亡,包括四名医生的死亡,迪文和其他人仍然致力于抗击这种疾病。刚果民主共和国卫生当局继续监测局势,强调需要改进个人防护装备(PPE)和遏制工作。暴发 仍然是一项重大的公共卫生挑战,卫生系统承受着巨大的压力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年11月17日。活动日期:Kinzi Divin 隔离 24 天。暴发 于 2026 年 5 月开始,持续至 2026 年 11 月。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国 (DRC),特别是北基伍省和贝尼市。受影响的人群包括医护人员,特别是医生和护士,以及受 暴发 影响的八个省份的普通人群。
事件
刚果民主共和国的 埃博拉病毒 暴发,Kinzi Divin 和 Muhindo Mbafumoja Shukuru 等医护人员在治疗患者时感染了病毒。暴发 已导致确诊病例和死亡,本迪布焦 埃博拉病毒 被确定为病原体。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年11月14日,刚果民主共和国已记录确诊病例11,376例,死亡5,568例。仅北基伍省就有2,860例确诊感染病例,1,590例死亡,病死率为55.6%。暴发 给卫生系统造成了巨大压力,并使一线工作人员面临高风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
暴发 仍在进行中,医护人员不顾个人风险继续与疾病作斗争。刚果民主共和国卫生当局正在发布情况报告,应对措施包括隔离、治疗 以及努力解决个人防护装备 (PPE) 短缺的问题。目前情况仍在调查中,需要采取进一步的遏制措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

After surviving Ebola, DR Congo doctors renew vow to fight the disease

News Brief
In the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), an ongoing Ebola outbreak has claimed over 5,500 lives since May 2026, with North Kivu Province being one of the hardest-hit areas. Kinzi Divin, a doctor who survived Ebola after 24 days of isolation, and nurse Muhindo Mbafumoja Shukuru, who contracted the virus through direct patient contact, highlight the risks faced by frontline workers. The outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo Ebola virus, has resulted in 11,376 confirmed cases and 5,568 deaths as of November 14, 2026, with a case fatality rate of 55.6% in North Kivu. Despite the personal toll, including the deaths of four doctors, Divin and others remain committed to combating the disease. The DRC's health authorities continue to monitor the situation, emphasizing the need for improved personal protective equipment (PPE) and containment efforts. The outbreak remains a significant public health challenge, with the health system under immense pressure.

World Gulf Middle East Asia Europe Africa Americas Business Qatar Business Middle East Business World Business Stock Market Energy Sports Qatar Sport Football Formula One NBA Cricket Tennis Rally Athletics Motosports Golf Horse Racing Olympics Padel Cuesports Views Editor-in-Chief Opinion Editorial Doha Today Community Campus Souq Life Style Technology Motoring Feature & more Wellness Food Travel Fashion Science & Environment Entertainment Cinema Music In Focus Qatar 2022 Doha Expo Asian Cup IAAF Doha2019 Ramadan Qatar National Day World Cup 2026 News Opinion Writers Videos Editorials Handbook World / Africa After surviving Ebola, DR Congo doctors renew vow to fight the disease Published: 17 Nov 2026 - 05:20 pm | Last Updated: 17 Nov 2026 - 05:31 pm File photo Follow usnews Copy A+ A- Xinhua BENI, DR Congo: After spending 24 days in isolation battling Ebola, Congolese doctor Kinzi Divin knows all too well the dangers of the disease he has been fighting on the front line. Having survived an infection that has already claimed the lives of several of his colleagues, the doctor has emerged from the ordeal with his determination intact. "We will continue. We will continue to fight this disease," Divin told Xinhua after being declared Ebola-free at an Ebola treatment center in Beni, a city in the eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). His recovery offers a rare moment of relief amid a devastating outbreak that has killed more than 5,500 people nationwide since May, while putting the doctors and nurses fighting to contain it at deadly risks. Six months after the DRC declared the outbreak in May, the country has recorded 11,376 confirmed Ebola cases, including 5,568 deaths, as of Nov. 14, according to the latest official figures. The epidemic, caused by the Bundibugyo Ebola virus, has spread to 68 health zones across eight provinces, becoming the world's second-largest Ebola outbreak on record. Divin recalled that his ordeal began with what seemed like an ordinary headache after a demanding day at work. "I contracted the disease while working. I was at work, and when I came back, I just felt a little strange," he said. "It started with headaches, and I thought it was normal given the pressure of work. But as time went by, I noticed that other symptoms were beginning to appear." When diarrhea hit, Divin realized that the symptoms could indicate something far more serious than exhaustion. With his instinct as a medical professional, he decided to seek isolation immediately. He spent 24 days at the treatment center before finally recovering. However, surviving Ebola has not erased the emotional toll of watching fellow health workers succumb to the same virus. "We have seen what happened to two of our colleagues who lost their lives. It was really stressful. This is something that must not be taken lightly," he said. "We are on the front line, and that exposes us to great risks," Divin said. In Mabalako, another Ebola-affected health zone in North Kivu Province, nurse Muhindo Mbafumoja Shukuru experienced a similar experience. Shukuru said he contracted the virus through direct contact with patients under his care. "As nurses, we are really in direct contact with patients, and it was through this contact that I became infected," he recalled. He initially experienced headaches, chills and fever. A blood test subsequently confirmed that he had contracted Ebola. While grateful to have survived, he remains deeply troubled by the virus's continued spread. "The resurgence of this disease is really something that makes no one happy," he said. The experiences of Divin and Shukuru underscore the dangers confronting medical personnel in North Kivu, one of the hardest-hit provinces in the current Ebola outbreak. According to Guy Mutombo, head of operations for the Ebola response in North Kivu, over 150 frontline health workers have contracted the virus in the province, including nine doctors, four of whom have died. Beyond the human toll, Mutombo said shortages of essential medical supplies, particularly personal protective equipment (PPE), remain a major obstacle to protecting frontline personnel. "We face many difficulties, and the first is related to supplies, particularly what we call PPE," he said. The challenge comes as North Kivu continues to report new Ebola infections and deaths, putting additional pressure on an already strained health system. According to the latest situation report released by the DRC's health authorities, North Kivu had recorded 2,860 confirmed infections and 1,590 deaths as of Nov. 14, representing a case fatality rate of 55.6 percent, higher than the national average of 48.9 percent. Six months after the DRC declared the Ebola outbreak on May 15, the virus continues to spread across the country, testing the resilience of health workers and the capacity of the national response. Divin, who has experienced Ebola both as a doctor and as a patient, knows that the risks facing his colleagues remain far from over. Yet neither the days spent in isolation nor the deaths of fellow doctors have persuaded him to abandon the fight. "We will continue to fight this disease," he said. Time
Publication date: 2026-11-17. Event date: 24 days of isolation for Kinzi Divin. The outbreak began in May 2026 and continued into November 2026.
Location and population
Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically North Kivu Province and Beni city. Affected population includes healthcare workers, particularly doctors and nurses, and the general population in the eight provinces impacted by the outbreak.
Event
Ebola virus outbreak in the DRC, with healthcare workers like Kinzi Divin and Muhindo Mbafumoja Shukuru contracting the virus while treating patients. The outbreak has resulted in confirmed cases and deaths, with the Bundibugyo Ebola virus identified as the causative agent.
Scale, impact and risk
As of November 14, 2026, the DRC has recorded 11,376 confirmed cases and 5,568 deaths. North Kivu Province alone has 2,860 confirmed infections and 1,590 deaths, with a case fatality rate of 55.6%. The outbreak has caused significant strain on the health system and exposed frontline workers to high risks.
Response, status and uncertainty
The outbreak is ongoing, with healthcare workers continuing to fight the disease despite personal risks. The DRC's health authorities are releasing situation reports, and the response includes isolation, treatment, and efforts to address shortages of personal protective equipment (PPE). The situation remains under investigation and requires further containment measures.

刚果民主共和国的埃博拉病毒 暴发 在应对措施实施六个月后仍处于失控状态 - 无国界医生组织

Ebola outbreak in DR Congo remains out of control, six months into the response - Doctors Without Borders
新闻简报据无国界医生组织 (MSF) 称,刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 的埃博拉病毒 暴发 在应对措施实施六个月后仍处于失控状态。尽管国际社会提供了大量资金,但物资、燃料和训练有素的人员等关键资源尚未到达受影响社区和一线工作人员手中。无国界医生报告称,已记录超过 11,000 例病例和超过 5,000 人死亡,约有 1,500 名患者入住无国界医生支持的设施。该组织强调了流行病学 监测 系统的严重缺陷,该系统未能有效识别大多数病例。这些缺点,包括未经培训和无薪的工作人员、缺乏车辆燃料以及落后的通讯基础设施,正在破坏遏制 暴发 的努力。无国界医生强调,迫切需要改进监督、人员配备和行动支持,以防止 暴发 进一步恶化。这种情况被描述为不可接受,需要立即采取行动解决当前的危机。
查看新闻五要素
时间
回复六个月后,发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 12 日。活动日期并未明确说明,但 暴发 自 2026 年 11 月 12 日起正在进行。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国 (DRC),特别是北基伍省。受影响人群包括社区、一线工作人员和确诊的埃博拉感染患者。
事件
刚果民主共和国(DRC)的埃博拉病毒 暴发 仍处于失控状态,无国界医生组织报告称,应对措施存在严重缺陷。暴发 被确认为 埃博拉病毒病 事件,社区和 治疗 中心报告了病例和死亡。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 病例已超过 11,000 例,死亡人数超过 5,000 人。大约 1,500 名确诊感染埃博拉病毒的患者已被无国界医生支持的机构收治。由于缺乏有效的应对措施和疾病的持续传播,风险被描述为高。
应对、状态与不确定性
该响应正在调查中,无国界医生强调了 监测 系统、资源分配和运营支持方面的重大缺陷。这种情况被描述为不可接受,需要紧急改进。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola outbreak in DR Congo remains out of control, six months into the response - Doctors Without Borders

News Brief
An Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) remains out of control six months into the response, according to Doctors Without Borders (MSF). Despite substantial international funding, critical resources such as supplies, fuel, and trained personnel are not reaching affected communities and frontline workers. MSF reports that over 11,000 cases and more than 5,000 deaths have been recorded, with approximately 1,500 patients admitted to MSF-supported facilities. The organization highlights severe gaps in the epidemiological surveillance system, which has failed to identify most cases effectively. These shortcomings, including untrained and unpaid staff, lack of fuel for vehicles, and poor communication infrastructure, are undermining efforts to contain the outbreak. MSF emphasizes the urgent need for improved oversight, staffing, and operational support to prevent the outbreak from spiraling further. The situation is described as unacceptable and requires immediate action to address the ongoing crisis.
