See latest updates Ebola outbreak in DR Congo remains out of control, six months into the response MSF teams are witnessing critical gaps as the response fails to reach communities. November 12, 2026 Share Share in FacebookShare in BlueSkyShare in LinkedInShare in TwitterShare in Mail Health care workers check each other's personal protective equipment to ensure proper fit before entering a high-risk area at an Ebola treatment center in North Kivu province. | DR Congo 2026 © Elio Parfait/MSF Related Democratic Republic of Congo Ebola Outbreaks and epidemics View more Related Democratic Republic of Congo Ebola Outbreaks and epidemics View more The second-largest Ebola disease outbreak in history remains out of control, six months after it was declared in Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), Doctors Without Borders/Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF) warns. Despite the considerable resources mobilized for the response, too little is reaching patients, frontline workers, and communities. Urgent action is needed to ensure those resources translate into effective, coordinated activities where they are needed most. Victoire, a patient with Ebola disease, visits with his wife Kasoki at the Ebola Treatment Center in Kitatumba, in Butembo, North Kivu province. | DR Congo 2026 © Elio Parfait/MSF Critical supplies and support are not reaching areas that need them Hundreds of millions of dollars have been disbursed by the international community, yet critical shortcomings in essential aspects of the response persist, undermining efforts to halt the outbreak and save lives. “Despite the vast amounts of money provided for the response, many frontline responders are left trying to stop the outbreak with empty hands and empty pockets,” says Dr. Javid Abdelmoneim, MSF International President. “Our teams are often left speechless by the dysfunction they witness in many areas. Six months into the response, this situation is simply unacceptable.” These dysfunctions take many forms: untrained and unpaid response personnel, some of whom have gone on strike; vehicles and ambulances without fuel; surveillance teams that exist on paper but are nowhere to be seen on the ground; community workers without phones or mobile data to share alerts; and health care staff left without the supplies or technical support needed to handle suspected cases. A health care worker decontaminates a colleague's PPE at an Ebola treatment center in North Kivu province. | DR Congo 2026 © Elio Parfait/MSF Surveillance infrastructure is woefully lacking One of our greatest concerns is the persistent failure of the epidemiological surveillance system, a cornerstone of any Ebola response. Despite the resources officially allocated to this critical activity, surveillance remains deeply problematic, with entire areas poorly covered or even completely neglected. Over the past month, close to two-thirds of reported Ebola-related deaths have occurred in communities. “Data collected by MSF illustrates just how serious the problem is,” says Abdelmoneim. “Since the beginning of the response, in Ituri province, only 9 percent of patients with Ebola disease treated by our teams had previously been identified as contacts by the surveillance system. Although community mistrust makes surveillance difficult, urgent improvements in oversight, staffing, and operational support are essential.” A health care worker supervises infection prevention and control procedures at an Ebola treatment center in North Kivu province. | DR Congo 2026 © Elio Parfait/MSF Key areas are being overlooked amid the response MSF is also concerned about the excessive concentration of efforts in certain areas, at the expense of other hotspots where needs are increasingly urgent. For example, in North Kivu province, the response has been too slow to match the scale of the epidemic, while Haut-Uélé province remains dramatically overlooked. In both provinces, MSF has observed teams that are inadequately resourced to respond effectively, as well as health facilities operating without adequate sterilization capacity, water, chlorine, or triage systems. This has turned some facilities into new centers of transmission, rather than places of care that contribute to stopping the outbreak. In the Beni health zone, North Kivu province, close to three-quarters of Ebola patients contracted the disease in a health facility. Six months into the response, MSF continues to receive requests for personal protective equipment (PPE) from health facilities and humanitarian organizations, many of which we cannot fulfil. This is not just a question of operational effectiveness — it is also a question of restoring trust among communities that have already paid an enormous price. Dr. Javid Abdelmoneim, MSF International President “Too few responders are operating beyond the initial hotspot to tackle the spread of the virus in other hard-hit parts of the country,” says Abdelmoneim. “Meanwhile, private health providers, such as clinics, hospitals, and pharmacies, as well as traditional healers, remain largely forgotten from the response plans. Yet they are often first point of contact for people seeking care — and too often where they get infected. The response must be adapted to this reality. Otherwise, we risk spending years constantly playing catch-up with this outbreak.” Over the past six months, the Congolese authorities and their international partners have achieved tangible results: Diagnostic capacity has been strengthened; isolation and treatment units have expanded; community engagement activities have increased; and trials have been launched to identify treatments and develop vaccines. Despite the vast amounts of money provided for the response, many frontline responders are left trying to stop the outbreak with empty hands and empty pockets. Dr. Javid Abdelmoneim, MSF International President Yet the shortcomings of the Ebola response remain profound and largely unaddressed, despite being widely acknowledged. The Congolese authorities have announced measures aimed at strengthening oversight and transparency in the activities carried out by the different stakeholders involved in the response in order to provide a clearer understanding of how resources are being allocated and used. Commitments and announcements must now translate into concrete change on the ground. With the outbreak surpassing 11,000 cases and over 5,000 deaths, the need for action is urgent. The Congolese government and United Nations agencies must ensure that the resources already mobilized translate into an effective response. This means strengthening oversight and accountability, ensuring that response personnel are properly trained, paid, equipped, and supported, and directing resources towards the areas where needs are greatest. “This is not just a question of operational effectiveness — it is also a question of restoring trust among communities that have already paid an enormous price,” says Abdelmoneim. Entrance of the Ebola Treatment Center in Kitatumba, Butembo, North Kivu province. | DR Congo 2026 © Elio Parfait/MSF How MSF is responding to the Ebola outbreak in DR Congo MSF was among the first organizations to support the Congolese authorities following the declaration of the outbreak on May 15, 2026. Over the past six months, MSF teams have been involved in setting up treatment centers and isolation facilities, strengthening infection prevention and control in health care facilities, conducting surveillance and community awareness activities, and sending rapid response teams to reach newly identified hotspots. As of early November, MSF's response covered five of the eight affected provinces. Teams continue to operate eight Ebola treatment centers, with three rapid response teams available. Over the past six months, approximately 1,500 patients with confirmed Ebola infections have been admitted to MSF-supported facilities. MSF has allocated more than $70 million to our Ebola response, funded almost entirely through private donations. Nearly 2,900 people, both working directly for or financially supported by MSF, are involved in these activities, approximately 90 percent of whom are locally recruited. 埃博拉病毒 暴发 病例达到 11,145 例,死亡 5437 人

Ebola Outbreak Reaches 11,145 Cases, 5437 Deaths
新闻简报据美国疾病控制和预防中心 (CDC) 数据,截至 2026 年 11 月 10 日,刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 埃博拉疫情确诊病例已达 11,145 例,死亡病例达 5437 例。这是有记录以来第二大 暴发,确诊病例在刚果民主共和国的下韦莱省、上韦莱省、伊图里省、北基伍省、南基伍省、南乌班吉省、北乌班吉省和乔波省。在刚果民主共和国以外,肯尼亚报告了 9 例病例和 2 例死亡,乌干达有 20 例确诊病例和 2 例死亡,加上 1 例可能病例和 1 例可能死亡,尼日利亚和法国各有 1 例确诊病例。疾病预防控制中心表示,美国公众和旅行者面临的风险较低,美国尚未报告病例。暴发 的传播速度比以前更快,CDC 积极参与应对工作,拥有 500 多名工作人员,其中有 120 多名部署到受影响国家。对其他病例的调查正在进行中,疑似病例暂时排除在计数之外,等待数据标准化。疾病预防控制中心还建议避免前往刚果民主共和国的某些省份旅行以降低风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至2026年11月10日,暴发确诊病例已达11145例,确诊死亡5437例。发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 15 日。对其他病例的调查正在进行中,疑似病例暂时排除在统计之外,等待数据标准化。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 确诊病例 11,114 例,死亡 5433 例。肯尼亚报告了 9 例确诊病例和 2 例死亡,乌干达报告了 20 例确诊病例和 2 例死亡,加上 1 例可能病例和 1 例可能死亡,尼日利亚和法国各有 1 例确诊病例。受影响人口包括刚果民主共和国下韦莱省、上韦莱省、伊图里省、北基伍省、南基伍省、南乌班吉省、北乌班吉省和乔波省的个人。
事件
刚果民主共和国由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉暴发确诊病例已达11,145例,确诊死亡人数为5437人。CDC 报告称,暴发 的传播速度比以前快得多。刚果民主共和国多个省份均已出现确诊病例,其中肯尼亚、乌干达、尼日利亚和法国均有确诊病例。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 目前是有记录以来第二大死亡病例,确诊死亡人数约占确诊病例的 49%。疾病预防控制中心认为美国公众和旅行者面临的风险较低,美国尚未报告病例。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心建议避免前往伊图里省和北基伍省,并避免前往上韦莱省和乔波省进行不必要的旅行。大约 500 名 CDC 工作人员参与了应对工作,其中 120 多名部署到受影响国家。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola Outbreak Reaches 11,145 Cases, 5437 Deaths

News Brief
As of November 10, 2026, the Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) has reached 11,145 confirmed cases and 5437 confirmed deaths, according to the CDC. This is the second-largest outbreak on record, with cases confirmed in DRC provinces such as Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu, Sud-Kivu, Sud-Ubangi, Nord-Ubangi, and Tshopo. Outside DRC, Kenya has reported 9 cases and 2 deaths, Uganda has 20 confirmed cases and 2 deaths plus 1 probable case and 1 probable death, and Nigeria and France have 1 confirmed case each. The CDC states that the risk to the American public and travelers is low, with no cases reported in the US. The outbreak is spreading faster than previous ones, and the CDC is actively involved in the response with over 500 staff, including more than 120 deployed to affected countries. Investigation of additional cases is ongoing, and suspected cases are temporarily excluded from the count pending data standardization. The CDC also recommends avoiding travel to certain DRC provinces to reduce risk.

Pylori Norovirus Healthcare Associated Infections (HAI) Prevention Antibiotics Immunizations Vaccines Respiratory Infections Community-Acquired Pneumonia Measles Influenza Sexually Transmitted Diseases HIV / AIDS HPV HSV PrEP Skin & Soft Tissue Diseases MRSA Zoonotic & Vector-Borne Diseases Lyme Disease ZIKA Choose Topic Spotlight - Vaccines in America| Antibiotics Deconstructed| Bench to Bedside With SIDP| Antimicrobial Stewardship| Hepatitis Advertisement News|Articles|November 15, 2026 Ebola Outbreak Reaches 11,145 Cases, 5437 Deaths Advertisement The Ebola outbreak caused by Bundibugyo virus in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) has reached 11,145 confirmed cases and 5437 confirmed deaths as of November 10, 2026, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).1 DRC accounts for 11,114 of those cases and 5433 deaths. The outbreak is now the second-largest Ebola outbreak on record and is spreading substantially faster than previous Ebola outbreaks, CDC reports.1 Outside DRC, Kenya has reported 9 confirmed cases and 2 deaths, Uganda 20 confirmed cases and 2 deaths plus 1 probable case and 1 probable death, and Nigeria and France 1 confirmed case each.1 No cases tied to this outbreak have been reported in the US, and CDC considers the overall risk to the American public and travelers low.1 Confirmed deaths represent about 49% of confirmed cases. Investigation of additional cases is ongoing, and suspected cases and deaths in DRC are temporarily excluded from the count pending data standardization, so totals are expected to change.1 How Fast Is the 2026 Ebola Outbreak Spreading? The outbreak surpassed 1000 confirmed cases within 40 days of response activation.1 By comparison, the 2018 Ebola outbreak in DRC took approximately 235 days to reach more than 1000 cases. This is the 17th Ebola outbreak in DRC.1 Cases have been confirmed in the DRC provinces of Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu (North Kivu), Sud-Kivu (South Kivu), Sud-Ubangi (South Ubangi), Nord-Ubangi (North Ubangi), and Tshopo.1 South Kivu has reported no confirmed cases since May 29. Frequently Asked Questions What is Bundibugyo virus? Bundibugyo virus is a type of Ebola virus and the cause of the 2026 outbreak in DRC. CDC reports it is the virus driving cases in DRC, Kenya, and Nigeria. Have any Ebola cases been reported in the US during this outbreak? No. As of November 13, 2026, CDC reports no Ebola cases associated with this outbreak in the US and considers the risk to the American public low. What travel restrictions are in place? Travelers who have been in DRC within 21 days of their flight cannot board commercial flights to the US. Travelers from Uganda or South Sudan must enter through designated airports for enhanced screening. Ebola Cases Reported Outside DRC As of October 23, Kenya's Ministry of Health has confirmed 9 cases of Ebola disease and 2 deaths.1 The initial case, reported October 6, involved a man who traveled from DRC through Uganda to Nairobi, where he was hospitalized and died in an isolation unit. Subsequent cases, including the second death, occurred among relatives and health care providers in close contact with him, all of whom were already in quarantine when diagnosed.1 Kenya's Ministry of Health has reported no community spread, and all cases were in Nairobi.1 On October 20, Nigeria reported its first case in the 2026 outbreak, with testing confirming Bundibugyo virus.1 Uganda reported cases at the same time DRC declared the outbreak, with 21 confirmed and probable cases and 3 deaths overall.1 The last confirmed case in Uganda recovered and was released from care on July 16, 2026, and France's single patient was released on July 4.1 Neither country has reported active cases. US Travel Restrictions and CDC Response US entry for travelers recently in DRC remains temporarily restricted.1 Travelers, including Americans, who have been in DRC within 21 days of their flight cannot board commercial flights to the US. US citizens and nationals who have been in Uganda or South Sudan, but not DRC, within 21 days of arrival must enter through designated airports for enhanced screening.1 Since 11:59 PM Eastern Standard Time on November 3, all passengers boarding an international flight to the US must attest they have not been in DRC during the 21 days before departure.1 CDC recommends avoiding all travel to Ituri and Nord-Kivu provinces and avoiding nonessential travel to Haut-Uele and Tshopo provinces. Approximately 500 CDC staff are involved in the response, including more than 120 people deployed to affected countries.1 Response Challenges for Clinicians and Public Health CDC cites limited health infrastructure, ongoing conflict, and frequent cross-border population movement as barriers to containment.1 Violence against health care workers and shortages of personal protective equipment are leading to infections among health care workers treating patients. Mistrust of government and misinformation further complicate outreach.1 For US clinicians, CDC directs providers to its clinical guidance for Ebola disease, screening and diagnosis resources for viral hemorrhagic fevers, and infection prevention and control recommendations for US hospitals.1 A travel history covering the prior 21 days remains central to identifying patients at risk. References Ebola Outbreak: Current Situation. CDC. Updated November 13, 2026. View five news elements
Time
As of November 10, 2026, the outbreak has reached 11,145 confirmed cases and 5437 confirmed deaths. The publication date is November 15, 2026. Investigation of additional cases is ongoing, and suspected cases are temporarily excluded from the count pending data standardization.
Location and population
The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) accounts for 11,114 cases and 5433 deaths. Kenya has reported 9 confirmed cases and 2 deaths, Uganda 20 confirmed cases and 2 deaths plus 1 probable case and 1 probable death, and Nigeria and France 1 confirmed case each. The affected population includes individuals in DRC provinces such as Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu, Sud-Kivu, Sud-Ubangi, Nord-Ubangi, and Tshopo.
Event
The Ebola outbreak caused by Bundibugyo virus in the DRC has reached 11,145 confirmed cases and 5437 confirmed deaths. The CDC reports that the outbreak is spreading substantially faster than previous ones. Cases have been confirmed in multiple DRC provinces, and there are confirmed cases in Kenya, Uganda, Nigeria, and France.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak is now the second-largest on record, with confirmed deaths representing about 49% of confirmed cases. CDC considers the risk to the American public and travelers low, with no cases reported in the US.
Response, status and uncertainty
CDC recommends avoiding travel to Ituri and Nord-Kivu provinces and nonessential travel to Haut-Uele and Tshopo provinces. Approximately 500 CDC staff are involved in the response, including more than 120 deployed to affected countries.
